Title: On the Complexity of Finding Decoherence Free Subspaces

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26769v1

Published Time: Wed, 23 Sep 2026 01:45:30 GMT

Number of Pages: 80

Markdown Content:
## On the Complexity of Finding Decoherence Free Subspaces 

Evan Borras 1, 2 

> 1

Center for Quantum Information and Control, University of New Mexico, Albuquerque, NM 87131, USA 

> 2

Department of Physics and Astronomy, University of New Mexico, Albuquerque, NM 87131, USA 

Decoherence free subspaces are a steady-state structure of the open quantum system which pre-serves quantum coherence between the states lying with in it and thus has found a variety of applications throughout quantum information science and technology. In this paper we study the computational complexity of deciding whether an open quantum system admits a decoherence free subspace or not. More specifically we study this problem with in the context of Markovian open quantum systems, governed by the time-independent Lindblad master equation. Along the way we introduce the k-Local Lindbladian problem, which captures the difficulty of computing purity decay rates under Lindbladian dynamics. We show that both problems are hard for the complexity class Quantum Merlin Arthur (QMA) when the locality k ≥ 5, with the first under perfect com-pleteness and the second being complete for QMA. Our hardness construction generalizes Kitaev’s clock Hamiltonian construction to the open quantum system setting by encoding the execution of a quantum circuit into the steady subspace of a Lindbladian containing both pure and mixed history states. This subspace is then mixed depending on the output of the encoded circuit. Our results suggest that deciding whether a generic Markovian open quantum system admits a decoherence free subspace is intractable even for quantum computation. 

I. INTRODUCTION 

The study of the ground state structure of closed quantum systems from a computational perspective has proven to be a compelling direction of research culminating in the creation of the field of Hamiltonian complexity [1, 2], which roughly speaking, is the study of quantum constraint satisfaction problems. Along with Kitaev’s landmark result showing the 5-Local Hamiltonian problem is QMA-Complete [3, 4], a multitude of other results have been shown [5–7] further cementing the comparison with the study of classical constraint satisfaction problems. In open quantum systems, ground state structure generalizes to become the steady-state structure of the dynamics. Given the fruitfulness of studying the ground state structure of the closed quantum system from a computational perspective, one may wonder what may come of studying the steady-state structure of open quantum systems from a computational perspective. As our model of an open quantum system, we consider systems governed by the time-independent Lindblad master equation [8] 

dρ dt = L(ρ) = −i[H, ρ ] +

, (1) where H denotes the system’s Hamiltonian, and the jump operators {Lμ}mμ=1 model the system’s interaction with its environment. Typically L is called a Lindbladian and the portion of L generated solely by the jump operator is called a Lindbladian dissipator. From a physical perspective Lindbladians generate uniformly continuous quantum Markov semigroups [9, 10] which describe the dynamics of an open quantum system coupled to a Markovian environment. From a computational standpoint, the Lindbladian is akin to a generator of a quantum mechanical continuous time Markov chain. The steady-state structure of time-independent Lindbladians has been analyzed in detail throughout [11–13] and [14]. Unlike the ground state structure of a Hamiltonian, all steady-states have the same eigenvalue of zero. Thus steady-state structure is typically classified according to how the dynamics drive the system into the steady-subspace. For reference on type of scheme see the discussion on steady-state structure in [14]. One of the steady-state structures unique to quantum mechanical systems is that of a decoherence free subspace. Roughly speaking, a decoherence free subspace is a subspace of pure quantum states that are steady under the dynamics generated by the Lindbladian. What makes a decoherence free subspace uniquely quantum mechanical is that in addition to the dynamics admitting pure steady-states, even the quantum mechanical coherences between these steady-states must also be steady under the dynamics. In essence the dynamics does not cause the system to decohere when initialized inside the decoherence free subspace. Decoherence free subspaces are an example of an information preserving structure of open quantum system dynamics [15] and thus have found many applications throughout quantum information science. Some examples include the use of decoherence free subspaces in the design of noise free quantum computation schemes [16] as well as designing autonomous quantum error error correction schemes [17]. arXiv:2609.26769v1 [quant-ph] 22 Sep 2026 2In this paper, we study the computational hardness of deciding whether a given Lindbladian has a decoherence free subspace. To make headway towards this goal, we consider the simpler problem of deciding whether a Lindbladian has a pure steady-state, or alternatively, if the Lindbladian admits a 1-dimensional decoherence free subspace. Thus, the hardness of deciding whether a Lindbladian has a decoherence free subspace should be at least as hard as it is to decide whether it admits a pure steady-state.  

> A. Complexity Theoretic Contributions

Throughout the rest of this work we introduce the following problems. 

• k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State (Definition 2): A decision problem, formalizing the notion of deciding whether a Lindbladian dissipator admits a 1-dimensional decoherence free subspace. 

• k-Local Lindbladian (Definition 3): A decision problem capturing the hardness of computing the purity decay rate under a given Lindbladian dissipator. 

• Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian : A variant of k-Local Lindbladian with the constraint that the Lind-bladian dissipator is generated by Hermitian jump operators. 

• Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian (Definition 4): A variant of the Separable k-Local Hamiltonian introduced in [18] under the additional restriction that in the (accept-case) the product state is conjugate symmetric across the system bi-partition. Our results can be summarized in FIG 1 depicting the various problems we introduce as well as the reductions between them. 

FIG. 1: Diagram of the problems we analyze and their inter-relations. Arrows denote reductions between problems where if we have A → B then A ≤ B.More succinctly, the highlights of what we have shown are listed below. 

• k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State is QMA 1-Hard for constant k ≥ 5. 

• k-Local Lindbladian is QMA-Complete for constant k ≥ 5. 

• Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian is QMA-Complete for constant k ≥ 5. The complexity class Quantum Merlin Arthur (QMA) serves a similar role in quantum complexity theory that the complexity class NP does in classical complexity theory. In essence, problems in QMA are easy to verify a solution to using quantum computation, but may or may not be hard to solve using quantum computation. Under the assumption that the complexity class of quantum computationally easy to solve problems, BQP, is not equal to QMA, our results suggest that the problem of deciding whether a Lindbladian admits a decoherence free subspace is intractable, even for quantum computation. 3 

> B. Technical Contributions

Along with the hardness results discussed above, some of our technical results that may be of independent use. More specifically we provide the following: 

• A novel quantum circuit to Lindbladian dissipator mapping. As an alternative to the construction built in [19] which admits a steady-subspace of only mixed history states of the quantum cirucit, our construction drives the system into a steady-subspace that includes both mixed history states and pure history states, thus allowing the ability to interpolate between the two. We analyze both the kernel and gap of our construction, therefore giving an upper-bound on the mixing time for the dynamics generated by our construction. For the definition of our construction see Definition 6 and for the details regarding its analysis see Appendix A. 

• A novel unary encoding Lindbladian dissipator that is Hermitian. This dissipator drives the system into a decoherence free subspace where the computational basis is encoded into unary. Such a construction could be built by leveraging some of the techniques used to stabilize the ground states of frustration-free Hamiltonians, see Theorem 5 of [20], but the construction would not produce a Lindbladian that is Hermitian with respect to the Hiblert-Schmidt inner product. Similar to our contribution above, we analyze the kernel as well as the gap of the Lindbladian dissipator, thus giving an upper-bound on the mixing time of its generated dynamics. For the details of our construction as well as its analysis see Appendix D. 

• A generalization of the projection lemma of Kempe et al. [5]. Similar to the original tool, our pure state decay projection lemma allows one to cut out parts of operator space, projecting our analysis into an easier to manage subspace. The primary difference is that our statement is adapted to apply to the pure state decay rate under the Lindbladian’s dynamics. Another major difference is that our tool allows one to project the analysis into a decoherence free subspace as opposed to the entire kernel. For the statement see Lemma 7 and for its proof see Appendix E. The paper is divided into five sections with the first being this introduction. In the following section we introduce notation that we will use throughout the paper as well as review some tools we will be using from complexity theory. The third section we discuss decoherence free subspaces as well as introduce our definition of the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem. In the fourth section we introduce the k-Local Lindbladian and cover its relation to the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem as well as its inclusion in the class QMA. Finally, in the fifth section, we show that the k-Local Lindbladian problem is QMA-Hard which allows us to show QMA 1-Hardness for the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem. We conclude with a discussion section, reviewing our results as well as providing future directions.  

> II. PRELIMINARIES

We begin by fixing some notation we will use throughout the paper. We will be working with finite dimensional quantum systems, and thus the pure state space can be described by a finite dimensional Hilbert space we will commonly denote as H. Subspaces of H will commonly be denoted by H with some subscript. The set of bounded linear operators over H will be denoted as B(H). If A ∈ B (H), then the Hermitian conjugate of A with respect to the inner product on H will be denoted by A∗, in-addition we will use standard bra-ket notation when denoting vectors in H and H∗ along with the inner product on H.The set B(H) forms a full Hilbert algebra with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product and standard matrix multiplication and addition. We denote the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product as ⟨• , •⟩ . Similarly, if R : B(H) 7 → B (H)and is a bounded linear operator, then we define the Hermitian conjugate of R with respect to ⟨• , •⟩ as R†. For bounded linear operators on B(H) we will typically use calligraphic font, while for bound linear operators on H we will typically use standard capital Latin-script. Continuing with the theme of comparison between H and B(H), we denote the operator norm with respect to H as ∥•∥ , while the operator norm with respect ⟨• , •⟩ over B(H) will be denoted as ∥•∥ ∞. We can also define the real and imaginary parts of an operator A ∈ B (H),

With ∥•∥ 1 fixed, we can define the trace distance between A ∈ B (H)and B ∈ B (H) as D(A, B ) = 12 ∥A − B∥1.Along with our notation discussed above, quantum states or density operators or density matrices will be used to refer to trace normalized postive-semidefinite operators in B(H), commonly denoted using Greek-script. > 1. Open Quantum Systems

In this paper we have restricted to open quantum systems modeled by the time-independent Lindblad master equation provided in equation (1). Before discussing the various useful properties about Lindbladian operators which will be used throughout the rest of this work, it is important to fix our definitions of locality for both Hamiltonians and Lindbladians. Let {Hi}ri=1 be a set of Hamiltonians, then H = Pri=1 is called k-local if each Hi acts non-trivially on at most k qubits. Similarly, if {Lμ}mμ=1 is a set of jump operators which generate a Lindbladian dissipator D, then D is called k-local if each Lμ acts non-trivially on at most k qubits. A k-local Lindbladian is simply one which is generated by a k-local Hamiltonian H and jump operators {Lμ}mμ=1 which generate a k-local dissipator D. In-addition, its also useful to fix our notion of a steady-state of a Lindbladian L, which is simply a quantum state ρ where ρ ∈ ker L.One of the main features of Lindbladians is that the real part of their spectrum is negative, see proposition 1 of [21]. Moreover Lindbladians are Hermitian preserving, i.e. any L satisfies L(A)∗ = L(A∗). Given the characterization of a Lindbladian’s spectrum we define the gap of a Lindbladian L as gap ( L) = inf

> 2. Complexity Theory

Given that the topic of this paper involves computational hardness we will be using some notions from complexity theory [23–25] throughout this work. Roughly speaking, complexity theory is concerned with how the computational resources required to solve a problem scale, as the size the problem increases. Typically the computational resources 6considered are time and space, which roughly correspond to the number of steps and size of memory an algorithm needs in-order to solve an instance of the problem. Usually an algorithm is called efficient if its computational resource costs scale as a polynomial of the size of the instance of the problem. An instance of a problem is formalized by a string x ∈ { 0, 1}∗ which encodes the data needed to solve the problem. The size of the instance of the problem x is then given by the length of the string x denoted |x|. The types of problems we will concern ourselves with are called decision problems where the output to the problem is to either accept or reject the instance of the problem. More specifically we consider the notion of a promise problem, where a promise problem L is a pair L = ( LA, LR) where LA, LR ⊆ { 0, 1}∗ and LA ∩ LR = ∅. The set LA contains the (accept-case) 

instances of the problem L while LR contains the (reject-case) instances and every instance x of a promise problem 

L is promised to be contained in either LA or LR.The class of promise problems we consider is the complexity class Quantum Merlin Arthur or QMA. Roughly speaking the class QMA formalizes the notion of problems that are easy for quantum computation to verify a solution to but may or may not be hard to find a solution for. In essence if a verifier, Arthur, has access to a prover, Merlin, which can provide a quantum state as proof of a solution for the problem, then Arthur can efficiently verify using quantum computation if the quantum state given by Merlin is a valid proof for the problem or not. Formally we define the class QMA below. 

Definition 1 (QMA) . A promise promblem L is in QMA if there exists a polynomial-time uniform family of quantum circuits Vx acting on register P of poly( |x|) qubits such that for every instance x of problem L,

• (accept-case) if x ∈ LA then there exists a quantum state |ψx⟩ ∈ H P such that Pr [accept | Vx |ψx⟩] ≥ b.

• (reject-case) if x ∈ LR then for all states |ψ⟩ ∈ H P , Pr [accept | Vx |ψ⟩] ≤ a.where b − a ≥ 1/ poly( n) and 0 < ϵ < a < b < 1 − ϵ with ϵ ≥ 1/2− poly( n). For b = 1 the class is denoted QMA 1.

Note, by polynomial-time uniform family of quantum circuits Vx we mean a family of quantum circuits Vx that can be constructed from an instance x using a classical algorithm in poly( |x|) time. Such a construction ensures that each Vx has a circuit size of poly( |x|) as well. In essence each circuit Vx can be compiled efficiently and has a circuit size that also scales efficiently with the size of the problem. In-addition, when the instance of the problem is in the (accept-case) then Arthur’s efficiently compiled and run quantum circuit can verify with high probability that the instance of the problem is to be accepted assuming Merlin provides the correct quantum proof. This is to be contrasted with the (reject-case) of the problem where Arthur is guaranteed to be able to verify that the instance of the problem is to be rejected with a low probability of error. Typically the (accept-case) condition is called the 

completeness condition of the class and the (reject-case) condition is called the soundness condition of the class. This naming convention follows from logic where we want true statements to have proofs while false statements to not have valid proofs. As an important side note, the class QMA 1 is included in QMA via the above definition, but it is unknown whether it equals QMA. Thus we must treat these two classes as potentially different complexity classes. For a good reference on quantum complexity theory see [26]. The final notion from complexity theory that we will need to review is the definition of a reduction or what is often called a polynomial-time many-one reduction. A problem L(1) can be reduced to a problem L(2) , denoted L(1) ≤ L(2) 

if there exists a polynomial-time classical algorithm that transforms every instance x of problem L(1) to an instance y

of problem L(2) where x ∈ L(1)  

> A

transforms into an instance y ∈ L(2)  

> A

and x ∈ L(1)  

> R

transforms into an instance y ∈ L(2)  

> R

.In essence, instances of L(1) in the (accept-case) transform into instances of L(2) in the (accept-case) and instances of L(1) in the (reject-case) transform into instances of L(2) in the (reject-case) under a reduction from L(1) to L(2) .Typically the condition that (accept-case) of L(1) transforms into an (accept-case) of L(2) is called the completeness 

of the reduction. Similarly, for the condition that an (reject-case) of L(1) transforms into a (reject-case) of L(2) is called the soundness of the reduction. Intuitively if L(1) ≤ L(2) then L(1) is as hard as L(2) since if we could solve L(2) 

efficiently, we could convert L(1) into L(2) efficiently while also preserving its (accept-case) and (reject-case) instances, then solve L(1) by apply our method to solve L(2) efficiently. Thus the whole procedure to solve L(1) via L(2) is efficient. This intuition is where we define our notion of QMA-Hardness from. A problem M is called QMA-Hard if for all L ∈ QMA then L ≤ M. Essentially the whole complexity class QMA is as hard as problem M. In-addition, M

is called QMA-Complete if M ∈ QMA as well. A similar notion for QMA 1-Hard and QMA 1-Complete can be defined as well. For references on complexity 7 

> III. DECOHERENCE FREE SUBSPACES

Intuitively a decoherence free subspace is a pure state subspace of the system’s Hilbert space H that does not feel the noise the environment induces on the system. Thus, if the environment induced dynamics is modeled by a Lindbladian L a decoherence free subspace HDFS is a subspace of H where the space B(HDF S ) is contained in the kernel of L. Decoherence free subspaces are a uniquely quantum mechanical steady-state structure of L since even the coherences between pure states that lie in the decoherence free subspace are also preserved under the dynamics of L. If the Lindbladian L can be cleanly written as a sum between a portion generated solely by a Hamiltonian H

and a dissipator D, then we typically ignore the portion of L generated by H as long as the Hamiltonian maps the decoherence free subspace of D onto itself [27]. Although the portion of L generated by the Hamiltonian H does not annihilate every operator in B(HDFS ), the dynamics does reduce to unitary dynamics on B(HDFS ) and thus can be accounted for when engineering control over the decoherence free subspace since no loss of coherence occurs. For our purposes we will consider B(HDFS ) to be contained inside the kernel of the entire Lindbladian, with the Lindlbadian’s we will consider being Lindbladian dissipators. If the Lindbladian L is given solely by a dissipator then we have the following useful Proposition from [27] providing a necessary and sufficient conditions for L to admits a pure steady-state. 

Lemma 4 (Proposition 2 in [27]) . Let D be a Lindbladian dissipator generated by jump operators {Lμ}mμ=1 , then 

D(|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|) = 0 if and only if for all μ ∈ [m], Lμ |ψ⟩ = cμ |ψ⟩ and Pmμ=1 L∗

> μ

Lμ |ψ⟩ = Pmμ=1 |cμ|2 |ψ⟩

One can see that requiring this statement to hold for all pure states with-in a subspace of H gives us a necessary and sufficient condition for when a Lindbladian dissipator admits a decoherence free subspace. In-addition, if we choose to restrict the jump operators used to generate the dissipator, we can further simplify the above condition, giving us the following Lemma. 

Lemma 5. Let D be a Lindbladian dissipator generated by jump operators {Lμ}mμ=1 where Lμ = L∗

Given that all decoherence free subspaces contain at least a single pure state, we can define a simplified version of the decoherence free subspace problem, described below. 

Definition 2 (k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State Problem) . Let D be a k-local Lindbladian dissipator acting on n qubits with associated Hilbert space H, given by jump operators {Lμ}m=poly( n) 

> μ=1

, with each jump operator having bounded operator norm ∥Lμ∥ ≤ poly( n) decide, 

• (accept-case) there exists a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ ∈ H such that D(|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|) = 0 ,

• (reject-case) for all pure quantum states |ψ⟩ ∈ H and all steady-states ρss ∈ B (H), D(|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ| , ρ ss ) ≥ 1/ poly( n),promised one of these to be the case. 

We define an instance of k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State as the set of jump operators J generating 

D as specified in Definition 2 The k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem asks one to decide whether a given Lindbladian has a pure steady-state or not. In the (accept-case) of the problem the Lindbladian has at least a single pure steady-state, and thus a decoherence free subspace of dimension one or greater. In the (reject-case) of the problem the Lindbladian cannot have a decoherence free subspace since all of the steady-states are guranteed to be mixed. Thus deciding whether a given Lindbladian admits a decoherence free subspace should be at least as hard as deciding whether it admits a single pure steady-state, ie deciding the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem. 8 

> IV. THE LOCAL LINDBLADIAN PROBLEM

The k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem introduced in Definition 2 provides us a simpler problem that encompasses the difficulty of deciding whether a Lindbladian dissipator has a decoherence free subspace. In-order to show that the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem is hard, we find it useful to introduce a new problem, for which it is easier to show hardness for, and can be reduced to the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem. We call this problem the k-Local Lindbladian , for which a restricted form of this problem can be reduced to an instance of the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem. Thus if we can show this restriced form of the k-Local Lindbladian problem is hard then we also can show that the general k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem is hard as well. Not only do find the introduction of the k-Local Lindbladian problem useful in our hardness argument for the 

k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem, but we also find that the general k-Local Lindbladian 

problem can be reduced to a type of Local Hamiltonian problem. Thus building an interesting bridge between problems regarding the steady-state structure of open quantum systems and that of the ground state structure of local Hamiltonians. In addition, this reduction allows us to show that the k-Local Lindbladian is QMA-Complete. We define our notion of the k-Local Lindbladian problem below. 

Definition 3 (k-Local Lindbladian Problem) . Let D be a k-local Lindbladian dissipator acting on n qubits with associated Hilbert space H, given by jump operators {Lμ}m=poly( n) 

> μ=1

, with each jump operator having bounded operator norm ∥Lμ∥ ≤ poly( n) decide, 

• (accept-case) there exists a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ ∈ H such that | ⟨| ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ| , Re D(|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|)⟩ | ≤ a,

• (reject-case) for all pure states |ψ⟩ ∈ H , | ⟨| ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ| , Re D(|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|)⟩ | ≥ b,promised one of these to be the case, where b − a ≥ 1/ poly( n).

We define an instance of k-Local Lindbladian as a tuple ( J, a, b ) where J denotes the set of jump operators generating D and a and b are the thresholds as specified in Definition 3. At first glance, the k-Local Lindbladian problem appears to resemble a local Hamiltonian problem more so than the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem does. From a physics perspective, the k-Local Lindbladian problem is concerned with the decay of the pure state manifold under the dynamics generated by D.To see this, consider calculating the decay rate of the purity at time t. We find, 

ddt [purity( ρ(t))] = ddt ⟨ρ(t), ρ (t)⟩ = ⟨ρ(t), (D + D†)[ ρ(t)] ⟩ = 2 |⟨ ρ(t), Re D(ρ(t)) ⟩| . (14) Thus, the k-Local Lindbladian problem asks us to decide if there exists a pure state with a purity decay rate slower than some threshold a, or if the entire pure state manifold decays faster than some threshold b.We also introduce our restricted version of the k-Local Lindbladian problem which is reducible to our pure steady-state problem. Formally we define the Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian problem as the k-Local Lind-bladian problem with the additional constraint that each jump operator Lμ in Definition 3 is Hermitian. An instance of Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian is defined similarly to that of k-Local Lindbladian , where the set of jump operators J in the instance ( J, a, b ) must satisfy the additional constraint. In-addition to Lemma 5 of Section III, the main tool used in our reduction of the Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian 

problem to the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem is what we call our distance to decay Lemma, which allows us to relate the trace distance from the steady-subspace to the decay under our Lindbladian. 

Lemma 6 (Distance to Decay Lemma) . Let L be a Lindbladian, for all S ∈ ker L and A ∈ B (H),

D(A, S ) ≥ 12 ∥L †∥∞

To carry this out, we show that any instance of Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian with a = 0 can be transformed efficiently into an instance of k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State that maps an (accept-case) or (reject-case) instance of Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian to the corresponding instance of k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State .

Proposition 1. Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian with a = 0 ≤ k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State .Proof. Let ( J, 0, b ) be an instance of Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian with a = 0. Since ( J, 0, b ) is a valid instance of Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian , we have that the set J generates a k-local Lindbladian dissipator, in-addition 

∥L∥ ≤ poly( n), therefore the set J forms a valid instance of k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State . Trans-forming ( J, 0, b ) to J can be done in constant time.

In-addition to the k-Local Lindbladian problem being connected to the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem, we can also connect it to a type of local Hamiltonian problem. More specifically, we show that we can reduce k-Local Lindbladian into a problem about finding product ground states of a local Hamiltonian that are symmetric, which we call the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem. From a complexity point of view, this reduction allows us to include the k-Local Lindbladian problem in QMA as the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem is a slight modification of the Separable k-Local Hamiltonian 

problem studied by Chaillox et al. in [18]. Our definition of Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian is provided below. 

Definition 4 (Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian Problem) . Let H be a 2k-local Hamiltonian acting on 2n qubits partitioned into n qubit registers A and B with H given by {Hi}r=poly( n) 

> i=1

where I ⪰ Hi ⪰ 0 and if Hi

acts non-trivially on k′ qubits in register A then it also acts non-trivially on k′ qubits in register B, decide 

• (accept-case) there exists a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ ∈ H A such that (⟨ψ|A ⊗ ⟨ ¯ψ|B )H(|ψ⟩A ⊗ | ¯ψ⟩B ) ≤ a,

• (reject-case) For all pure quantum states |ψ⟩ ∈ H A and |ϕ⟩ ∈ H B , (⟨ψ|A ⊗ ⟨ ϕ|B )H(|ψ⟩A ⊗ | ϕ⟩B ) ≥ b

promised one of these to be the case, where b − a ≥ 1/ poly( n).

We define an instance of Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian as the tuple ( F, a, b, A, B ) where F

denotes the set of Hamiltonians which sum to H and a, b, A, and B are specified as in Definition 4. From a physics point of view, the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem captures the difficulty of computing ground state energies of a local Hamiltonian under the additional constraint that the ground state must be both a product state and conjugate symmetric across a bi-partition of the system. We have the following proposition regarding the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem. 

Proposition 2. For k ≤ log( n), Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian ∈ QMA 11 

Proof. See Appendix H Intuitively the connection between k-Local Lindbladian and Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian 

can be made by viewing the k-Local Lindbladian problem from the perspective of the operator-vector correspon-dence of B(H). In the doubled Hilbert space representation of B(H) given by the operator-vector correspondence, mixed states ρ ∈ B (H) become un-normalized entangled states while pure states |ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ| ∈ B (H) become conjugate symmetric product states. In-addition, Re D becomes a simple bounded linear operator acting on the doubled Hilbert space. Thus, the problem of finding a pure state with a decay rate below some threshold becomes equivalent to finding a product state on the doubled Hilbert space with an “energy” below the same threshold. This argument is formalized in our proof of the following Lemma. 

Proposition 3. For k ≤ log( n), k-Local Lindbladian ≤ Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian .Proof. Since |F | = m = poly( n)and we can compute each Hμ ∈ F in poly( n) time, we also can compute the whole set F in poly( n) time as well. Thus we can efficiently construct an instance of Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian from an instance of k-Local Lindbladian for k ≤ log( n).

Corollary 1. For k ≤ log( n), k-Local Lindbladian ∈ QMA 14  

> V. HARDNESS OF THE LOCAL LINDBLADIAN PROBLEM

In order to show that the k-Local Lindbladian problem is QMA-Hard we must be able to reduce a problem that is already QMA-Hard problem to k-Local Lindbladian . Given the structure of the problem it may seem natural to choose a QMA-Hard local Hamiltonian problem, such as Separable k-Local Hamiltonian [18], to transform into our Lindbladian problem. Taking such an approach, we can map Re L into a Hamiltonian acting on a doubled Hilbert space, but the symmetry that Lindbladians require in-addition to the pure state constraint on the problem make the reduction not clear. Instead we choose to generalize the approach taken by Kitaev in [3, 4] to show QMA-Hardness for the local Hamiltonian problem through reducing from Quantum Circuit-SAT defined below. 

Definition 5 (Quantum Circuit-SAT Problem) . Let V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 be a quantum circuit acting on a proof register M of n qubits and an ancillary register A of k = poly( n) qubits, decide 

• (accept-case) there exists an n qubit state |ψ⟩ such that Tr  |1⟩ ⟨ 1|M0 V |ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|M ⊗ | 0⟩ ⟨ 0|A V ∗ ≥ b

• (reject-case) for all n qubit states |ψ⟩, Tr  |1⟩ ⟨ 1|M0 V |ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|M ⊗ | 0⟩ ⟨ 0|A V ∗ ≤ a

promised one of these to be the case, where b − a ≥ 1/ poly( n).

We define an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT as a tuple ( V, b, a ) where V is a quantum circuit and b, a ∈ [0 , 1] with V , b and a satisfying their respective conditions in Definition 5. By Defintion 1 Quantum Circuit-SAT is QMA-Complete. Thus, if we can efficiently transform an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT into an instance of k-Local Lindbladian that maps the (accept-case) and (reject-case) of Quantum Circuit-SAT to their respective cases for k-Local Lindbladian then we have shown k-Local Lindbladian to be QMA-Hard. Similar to Kitaev’s Hamiltonian construction [3, 4], from the quantum circuit 

V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0, we encode the correct execution of the circuit into history states, quantum states on a larger Hilbert space encoding the full computation an input state undergoes under the quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0.We then engineer a Lindbladian dissipator that “checks” the correct execution of the circuit by driving the system into a steady-state space consisting of only the history states associated to the quantum circuit. We then build a Lindbladian dissipator that preserves the purity of this steady-state space if the output of the circuit is that of an 

(accept-case) of Quantum Circuit-SAT , mixing the steady-state space of the system if it is not. 

FIG. 2: A visualization of our Quantum Circuit-SAT disispator construction. 15 We start by designing the dissipator which checks the correct propagation of the quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0,by driving the system into a steady-state subspace of history states. It is important to note that at this stage we are not sure whether the quantum circuit we are given is an (accept-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT or a 

(reject-case) instance. Thus the steady-state subspace must contain both history states that are pure and ones that are mixed. Luckily we have notions of a history state that fit both of these constraints. We define a pure history state associated to quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 acting on Hilbert space H with input state |ϕ⟩ ∈ H as the quantum state |ηϕ⟩ ∈ H ⊗ H C , where

More specifically, given a quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0, the corresponding dissipator D(V )prop should drive the system into a steady-state subspace containing only the pure and mixed history states associated to V . To do this, we break our dissipator into terms Dt where each term Dt “checks” the correct propagation of the gate Ut in our circuit. Interestingly, to construct each term Dt, we simply take the Hamiltonian term Ht used in Kitaev’s Hamiltonian construction [4], and re-appropriate it as jump operator generating a Lindbladian dissipator. Formally, we define our propagation dissipator below.

To “check” the output of the circuit we require a dissipator that mixes the steady-state subspace of the propagation dissipator when the quantum circuit is associated to a (reject-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT and conversely, preserves the steady-state subspace of the propagation dissipator when circuit is associated to an (accept-case) instance of the problem. If the 0 th qubit of the proof register 

M0 is defined as the output qubit of Quantum Circuit-SAT , then the Lindbladian dissipator generated by the jump operator |0⟩ ⟨ 0|M0 ⊗ | T − 1⟩ ⟨ T − 1|C realizes this described behavior. Similarly, we can “check” the initialization of the i-th ancillary qubit Ai, using the Lindbladian dissipator generated by the jump operator |1⟩ ⟨ 1|Ai ⊗ | 0⟩ ⟨ 0|C . Thus we can define the full Lindbladian dissipator which embeds the Quantum Circuit-SAT problem below.

(57) The main tool we leverage in our prove of hardness for the k-Local Lindbladian problem is a generalization of the projection lemma of Kemp et al. [5] which we use when lower-bounding the decay a pure state undergoes when evolving under our Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator. Similar to the original projection lemma, our pure state decay projection lemma permits us to cut out parts of B(H) that decay quickly under our Lindbladian dissipator. This allows us the luxury of focusing our analysis on a smaller subset of B(H), with the important addition that this smaller subset contains only pure quantum states.

Proof. See Appendix E 17 The argument that the k-Local Lindbladian problem is QMA-Hard roughly breaks up into two parts. In the first part, we show that our dissipator of Definition 7 yields a reduction from Quantum Circuit-SAT to log( n)-Local Lindbladian . In the second half, we add additional qubits into our clock register and encode the circuit’s clock into unary. This allows us to replace the quasi-local jump operator terms with k-local ones. This modification of the jump operators forces the steady-subspace of the dissipator to allow for illegal clock register states. To circumvent this we add an additional dissipator which forces these operators to decay rapidly, letting us use our pure state decay projection lemma to cut-off this portion of B(H). Inside this subspace the k-local Lindbladian dissipator can be mapped to our quasi-local QMA-Hard dissipator, allowing us to re-apply the analysis from the first part. To show that the dissipator of Definition 7 yields an appropriate reduction from Quantum Circuit-SAT to log( n)-Local Lindbladian we must first show that the jump operators which generate the dissipator of Definition 7 can be efficiently construct from an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT , in-addition to forming an appropriate instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian . We then require showing the completeness and soundness of the reduction. In essence, given an (accept-case) or (reject-case) of Quantum Circuit-SAT the corresponding instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian constructed is the appropriate (accept-case) or (reject-case) . For completeness, we simply show that the pure history state associated to a high accept probability input state of Quantum Circuit-SAT has the appropriate decay under the dissipator associated to an (accept-case) of Quantum Circuit-SAT . To show this we leverage the following lemma, which relates the decay of a pure history state with input state |ϕ⟩, to the probability that the Quantum Circuit-SAT circuit V rejects this input state. 

Lemma 8. Let (V, b, a ) be an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT and D(V,b,a ) defined according to equation (54) 

of Definition 7. Let |ηϕ⟩ be a pure history state associated to V with input state |ϕ⟩ ⊗ | 0⟩A ∈ H M ⊗ H A, then the decay of |ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ| under D(V,a,b ) is given by 

| ⟨| ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ| , Re D(V,a,b )(|ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ|)⟩ | =

See Appendix F For soundness we must argue when given a (reject-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT , that all pure states have a decay under the associated Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator that is lower-bounded by some non-zero value, ie there does not exist pure steady-states. To argue this, let D(V,b,a ) be a Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator acting on B(H) and consider the, increasing in size, family of subspaces Hcirc ⊆ H prop ⊆ H . Roughly speaking, Hprop is the subspace of H consisting of pure history states associated to the quantum circuit V . Similarly, Hcirc corresponds to the subspace of Hprop where the ancillary register A of the quantum circuit V is initialized in |0⟩A. In essence, the pure history states in Hcirc have their input states properly initialized. Our argument proceeds by using the pure state decay projection lemma to “project” us through this family of pure state subspaces until we reach Hcirc . Inside Hcirc ,we can calculate the decay by leveraging the fact that D(V,b,a ) is associated to a (reject-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT .Before proceeding it will be useful to provide formal definitions of our pure state subspaces Hprop and Hcirc .Furthermore, it will be necessary to prove that our definitions do indeed realize the intuitive descriptions of Hprop 

and Hcirc provided above. Below we provide our formal definition of the history state subspace Hprop .

Definition 8 (History State Subspace) . Let (V, b, a ) be an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT and D(V )prop acting on B(H) be defined according to equation (50) of Definition 6, then we define 

Hprop = span

Since H1 functions like a decoherence free subspace of the dissipator, roughly speaking, what we are trying to control, is the overlap between the orthogonal complement of the dissipators decohernece free subspace and its kernel. This leakage out of M into the kernel inhibits the strength of the dissipator’s gap. This could potentially reduce the strength of the dissipator’s gap to be arbitrarily small, thus preventing our ability to control the perturbation to the pure state decay. In the following statements we bound how bad this leakage can be for each of the dissipators involved in our Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator.

Theorem 1. log( n)-Local Lindbladian is QMA -Hard .Proof. To show log( n)-Local Lindbladian is QMA-Hard we show there exists a reduction from Quantum Circuit-SAT to log( n)-Local Lindbladian .Let ( Q, b, a ) be an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT . Our reduction is two steps. From ( Q, b, a ) we con-struct an equivalent Quantum Circuit-SAT instance ( V, b, a ), then we construct our log( n)-Local Lindbladian 

instance ( J, a ′, b ′). To do this note, we can always transform Q = QT ′−1QT −2 · · · Q0 into an equivalent circuit 

V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 that outputs the same quantum state at the end of the computation by simply adding identity gates into the circuit Q. Thus we transform ( Q, b, a ) into an equivalent instance ( V, b, a ) by adding identity gates into the quantum circuit Q = QT ′−1Q′

to produce quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 where 

T = T ′ + 2 − ab − a + 2 (72) From ( V, b, a ), let D(V,b,a ) be defined according to equation (54) of Definition 7. We construct our instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian by letting J be a set of jump operators generating D(V,b,a ) and setting

(77) 20 which generate Γ in Din , Γ out Dout , and D(V )prop respectively. If we set J equal to the set of all jump operators in Jin ,

Jout , and Jprop , then J generates D(V,b,a ).Let L ∈ J, we have ∥L∥ ≤ poly( T, k ), which follows from each of the jump operators in ˜Jin , ˜Jout , and ˜Jprop 

having an operator norm upper-bounded by a constant, and equation (57) of Definition 7. Since T ′ = poly( n), equation (72) implies T = poly( n) too. In-addition k = poly( n), thus ∥L∥ ≤ poly( n) as required. Similarly, each L

is acts non-trivially on at most log( T ) qubits given that each operator |t′⟩ ⟨ t|C acts on log( T ) many qubits. Since we have T = poly( n), we have that each L acts non-trivially on at most log( n) qubits implying J generates a log( n)-local Lindbladian dissipator as required. Finally, since both T = poly( n) and k = poly( n), there are poly( n) many jump operators in the set J, thus ( J, a ′, b ′) forms a valid instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian .To complete the description of our reduction we require the algorithm that constructs ( J, a ′, b ′) from our instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT (Q, b, a ) to be efficient. Equations (72) and (73) imply we can compute a′ and b′ in poly( n) time. In-addition, the transformation from Q to V requires only adding (2 − a)/(b − a) = poly( n) many identity gates into the description of the circuit Q which can also be done in poly( n) time. Finally, note each of the jump operators in Jin and Jout are matrices of dimension poly( n) × poly( n) and thus can be computed in poly( n)time from the dimension poly( n) × poly( n) matrix representations of |1⟩ ⟨ 1|Ai , |0⟩ ⟨ 0|M0 , and |t′⟩ ⟨ t|C using standard matrix manipulation algorithms. A similar argument holds for each of the jump operators in Jprop since each of the unitary gates Ut in the quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 act on at most a constant number of qubits. Since there are poly( n) many jump operators in J, and each L ∈ J can be computed in poly( n) time, the total runtime of the algorithm computing the set of jump operators J from ( V, b, a ) is poly( n) which is efficient. Thus there exists an effi-cient algorithm transforming our instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT to our instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian .

Completeness: 

Let ( Q, b, a ) be an (accept-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT , and ( J, a ′, b ′) be the corresponding instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian generated according to the description above. The jump operators J generate D(V,b,a )

defined in equation (54) of Definition 7 where ( V, b, a ) is also an (accept-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT 

since both circuits Q and V output the same quantum state. For completeness, we require ( J, a ′, b ′) to be an (accept-case) instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian , which requires there to exist a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ such that 

| ⟨| ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ| , Re D(V,b,a )(|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|)⟩ | ≤ a′. (78) To show this, consider a pure history state |ηϕ⟩ associated to the quantum circuit V . Choosing |ϕ⟩ = |ψ⟩M ⊗ | 0⟩A

and applying Lemma 8 we find

Theorem 2. log( n)-Local Lindbladian is QMA -Complete In-addition, the proof of Theorem 1 also allows us to show a hardness result for log( n)-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State .

Theorem 3. log( n)-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State is QMA 1-Hard 

Proof. Consider the proof of Theorem 1 and the set of jump operators J used to form the QMA-Hard instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian . For all L ∈ J, it follows from equations (75) (76) and (77) that L = L∗. Thus the QMA-Hard instance of log( n)-Local Lindbladian is also a valid instance of Hermitian log( n)-Local Lindbladian .Therefore Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian is QMA-Hard using the proof of Theorem 1. In our instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT (Q, b, a ) in the proof of Theorem 1 set b = 1. Thus Hermitian 

log( n)-Local Lindbladian with a = 0 is QMA 1-Hard. From Proposition 1 of Section III we find log( n)-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State is QMA 1-Hard. The quasi-localness of the dissipator used to show the hardness result of Theorem 1 comes from the portions of the jump operators involving the clock register C. To extend the result of Theorem 1 to apply to the k-Local Lindbladian problem with constant k we must modify our clock register in such a way as to allow the quasi-local terms in the Lindbladian to become local. To do this we take the standard approach of encoding the quantum circuit’s clock in unary as opposed to binary. In essence, we map 

|t⟩ 7 → | 0⟩⊗T −t ⊗ | 1⟩⊗t , (99) 23 implying our clock register C goes from containing log( T ) qubits to containing T qubits. Since our clock register contains T qubits, there exists T -bit strings that no longer encode a time t. Therefore we define S(T )legal = 0T −t1t Tt=0 and S (T )illegal = {0, 1}T \ S(T )legal (100) where S (T )legal denotes the T -bit strings that encode a valid time t in unary. Our clock register’s Hilbert space also decomposes as HC = H(T )legal ⊕ H (T )illegal where we have defined 

H(T )legal = span 



{| x⟩} x∈S(T )legal 



and H(T )illegal = span 



{| x⟩} x∈S(T )illegal 



. (101) Applying the mapping described in equation (99) to equations (48) and (49) give us our new definitions of a pure history state and a mixed history state respectively, but applying this mapping to Definition 7 does not gives us a Lindbladian dissipator with jump operators that act locally. To fix this, if we assume our clock register C is constrained to exist only in valid unary clock states then we can map 



|t⟩ ⟨ t| 7 → | 01 ⟩ ⟨ 01 |t+1 ,t , |t + 1 ⟩ ⟨ t| 7 → | 011 ⟩ ⟨ 001 |t+2 ,t +1 ,t ,

|T − 1⟩ ⟨ T − 1| 7 → | 1⟩ ⟨ 1|T −1 , |T − 1⟩ ⟨ T − 2| 7 → | 11 ⟩ ⟨ 01 |T −1,T −2 ,

|0⟩ ⟨ 0| 7 → | 0⟩ ⟨ 0|1 , |1⟩ ⟨ 0| 7 → | 01 ⟩ ⟨ 00 |2,1 ,

(102) inside Definition 7, resulting in a Lindbladian dissipator with jump operators that do act locally. Performing this mapping creates a new problem that arises from the fact that the mapping described in equation (102) is only valid within B(H(T )legal ). To deal with this new problem we add an additional Lindbladian dissipator Γ unary Dunary that drives the system into this subspace. Interestingly, in-order to engineer Γ unary Dunary we must diverge from the approach previously taken. In our previous approach we would have taken the Hamiltonian Hstab = PT −2 

> t=0

|10 ⟩ ⟨ 10 |t+1 ,t , used by Kiteav et al. in [4] to stabilize the legal unary clock subspace, and turned the individual Hamiltonian constraints |10 ⟩ ⟨ 10 |t+1 ,t into jump operators generating Dunary . The problem with this approach is that the kernel of this Dunary contains pure illegal clock register states. To see this note that the pure illegal clock register states can still be eigenvectors of all the jump operators 

|10 ⟩ ⟨ 10 |t+1 ,t . Applying Lemma 5 implies those states also lie in the kernel of the dissipator. To construct Γ unary Dunary consider the subspace of the clock register C given by qubits Ct+2 ,t +1 ,t = {t + 2 , t + 1 , t }.Defining our local illegal and legal bit strings as Sloc illegal = {000 , 001 , 011 , 111 } and S loc legal = {100 , 010 , 101 , 110 }, (103) allows us to partition HCt+2 ,t +1 ,t = Hloc legal ⊕ H loc illegal where we have defined, 

Hloc legal = span 



{| x⟩} x∈Sloc legal 



and Hloc illegal = span 



{| x⟩} x∈Sloc illegal 



. (104) We require a set 3-local jump operators that collectively have each Hloc legal as an eigenspace but not Hloc illegal . As a side note, for a single Hermitian jump operator this is not possible since part of the spectrum would have to be assigned to vectors in Hloc illegal . In addition, if we had considered to a 2-qubit subspace instead of 3-qubit, then the local illegal subspace would be 1-dimensional. This forces every operator which has the local legal subspace as an eigenspace, too also have the local illegal subspace as an eigenspace. Thus we are forced to consider the 3-local jump operators as a starting point with more than one jump operator in the set. Define our set of 3-local jump operators acting on register Ct+2 ,t +1 ,t as J(t). Take for all L ∈ J(t), L ∈ B (Hloc illegal )as our ansatz. Clearly, Hloc legal is a collective eigenspace with eigenvalue 0 for all jump operators in J(t). Thus, we only require that none of the vectors in Hloc illegal are eigenvectors of all the jump operators in J(t) simultaneously. In order to satisfy this condition, we can simply take J(t) to be the set of jump operators generating global depolarization on the subspace B(Hloc illegal ). The full Dunary becomes the dissipator generated by the jump operators in each J(t) for t ∈ { 0, 1, · · · , T − 3}. Since each 3-qubit window of a valid unary clock state lies in B(Hloc legal ) we immediately have that B(H(T )legal ) is contained in the kernel of Dunary . In addition , since the local depolarization dissipators overlapped, outside of B(H(T )legal ) the system is driven into the maximally mixed state supported on H(T )illegal . Thus the only pure steady-states lie in B(Hlegal ) with the addition that leakage outside of B(H(T )legal ) is small since the only steady-state supported on both portions of the kernel is maximally mixed state. Therefore, we can define our complete local dissipator which embeds Quantum Circuit-SAT , given below. 24

D(V,b,a )loc = Γ unary Dunary + ˜D(V,b,a ), (105)

Theorem 4. For k ≥ 5, k-Local Lindbladian is QMA -Hard .Proof. To show k-Local Lindbladian is QMA-Hard we show there exists a reduction from Quantum Circuit-SAT to k-Local Lindbladian for k ≥ 5. Let ( Q, b, a ) be an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT . Similar to our reduction in Theorem 1, from ( Q, b, a ) we construct an instance of k-Local Lindbladian by transforming the Quantum Circuit-SAT instance ( Q, b, a )into an equivalent instance ( V, b, a ) then into the k-Local Lindbladian instance ( J, a ′, b ′) for k ≥ 5. Sim-ilarly, to transform ( Q, b, a ) into an equivalent instance ( V, b, a ) we add identity gates into the quantum circuit 

Q = QT ′−1QT ′−2 · · · Q0 to produce quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 where 

T = T ′ + 3 − ab − a + 2 . (113) From Quantum Circuit-SAT instance ( V, b, a ), let D(V,b,a )loc be the local Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator defined in equation (105) of Definition 10. We construct our instance of k-Local Lindbladian by letting J be a set of jump operators which generate D(V,b,a )loc and setting 

(

> T

. (114) Similar to our proof of Theorem 1 we confirm, 

b′ − a′ = (b − a)T − (3 − a)

T = (b − a)( T ′ + 2) 

T ′ + 3−ab−a + 2 ≥ 1poly( n) . (115) Thus we only require the set of jump operators J to satisfy the conditions of the problem in-order for ( J, a ′, b ′) to be a valid instance of k-Local Lindbladian .According to equation (105) of Definition 10, D(V,b,a )loc = Γ unary Dunary + ˜D(V,b,a ) where Dunary is generated by jump operators ˜Junary =

Equation (54) of Definition 7 implies we can write ˜D(V,b,a ) = Γ out ˜Dout + Γ prop ˜D(V )prop +Γin ˜Din where ˜Din , ˜D(V )prop , and ˜Dout are equal to Din , D(V )prop , and Dout of equation (54) after having applied the mapping of equation (102). 

Junary = {pΓunary L | L ∈ ˜Junary },Jin = {√Γin L | L ∈ ˜Jin },Jout = {√Γout L | L ∈ ˜Jout },Jprop = {pΓprop L | L ∈ ˜Jprop }

(119) 26 which generate Γ unary Dunary , Γ in ˜Din , Γ out ˜Dout , and Γ prop ˜D(V )prop respectively. Thus D(V,b,a )loc can be generated by the set of jump operators J, equal to the set of all jump operators contained in the sets Junary , Jin , Jout , and Jprop .Note, each of the jump operators in ˜Junary , ˜Jin , ˜Jout and ˜Jprop have an operator norm upper-bounded by a constant. In-addition, all of the dissipation rates are poly( T, k ), thus for all L ∈ J we have ∥L∥ ≤ poly( n). We also find, since each of the jump operators in ˜Junary , ˜Jin , and ˜Jout act on k ≤ 3 qubits, each of the jump operators in Junary , Jin , and 

Jout also act on k ≤ 3 qubits. Since each gate Ut in our quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 acts on k ≥ 2, we find that each jump operator in Jprop acts on k ≥ 5 qubits. Thus for all L ∈ J, the jump operator L acts non-trivially on at most k qubits for k ≥ 5 as required. In-addition, since T = poly( n) and k = poly( n), we have poly( n) many jump operators in J. Thus( J, a ′, b ′) forms a valid instance of k-Local Lindbladian for k ≥ 5. To complete our description of the reduction we require the existence of an efficient algorithm that can construct (J, a ′, b ′) from our instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT (Q, b, a ). Equations (113) and (114) imply we can compute a′

and b′ in poly( n) time. In-addition, the transformation from Q to V only requires adding [(3 −a)/(b−a)]+2 = poly( n)many identity gates into the description of the circuit Q which can also be done in poly( n) time. Since each of the jump operators in J are k-local for a constant k ≥ 5, they can be represented as matrices of constant dimension and can be computed in constant time from the matrix representations of their various components. Since there are poly( n) many jump operators in J, the total runtime of the algorithm computing the set of jump operators J from (V, b, a ) is poly( n) which is efficient. Thus there exists an efficient algorithm transforming an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT to an instance of k-Local Lindbladian for k ≥ 5. 

Completeness: 

Let ( Q, b, a ) be an (accept-case) instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT , and ( J, a ′, b ′) be the corresponding instance of k-Local Lindbladian generated according to the description above. The jump operators J generate D(V,b,a )loc 

defined in equation (105) of Definition 10 where ( V, b, a ) is also an (accept-case) instance Qunatum Circuit-SAT .For completeness, we require ( J, a ′, b ′) to be an (accept-case) instance of k-Local Lindbladian implying there exists a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ such that 

| ⟨| ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ| , Re D(V,b,a )loc (|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|)⟩ | ≤ a′. (120) To show this, consider a pure history state |ηϕ⟩ associated to V . Choosing |ϕ⟩M A = |ψ⟩M ⊗ | 0⟩A and applying Lemma 15 we find 

| ⟨| ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ| , Re D(V,b,a )loc (|ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ|)⟩ | =

Theorem 5. For k ≥ 5, k-Local Lindbladian is QMA -Complete Similarly we also find, a hardness result for the local version of our pure steady-state problem. 

Theorem 6. For k ≥ 5, k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State is QMA 1-Hard 

Proof. Consider the proof of Theorem 4 and the set of jump operators J used to form the QMA-Hard instance of 

k-Local Lindbladian . For all L ∈ J, it follows from equations (116), (117), (118), and (119) that L = L∗. Thus the QMA-Hard instance of k-Local Lindbladian is also a valid instance of Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian .Therefore for k ≥ 5, Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian is QMA-Hard using the proof of Theorem 4. In our instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT (Q, b, a ) in the proof of Theorem 4 set b = 1. Thus for k ≥ 5, 

Hermitian k-Local Lindbladian with a = 0 is QMA 1-Hard. From Proposition 1 of Section III we find for k ≥ 5, 

k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State is QMA 1-Hard. 28  

> VI. DISCUSSION

In this work we have studied the computational complexity of deciding whether a given open quantum system, modeled by a Lindbladian, has a decoherence free subspace. To make headway towards this goal, we introduced and analyzed three problems: the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem, the k-Local Lindbladian 

problem, and the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem, along with their interrelations depicted in FIG 1. Our main results establish that for k ≥ 5, the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem is QMA 1-Hard with the addition that the k-Local Lindbladian problem is QMA-Complete. Since the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem is concerned with deciding whether a given Lindbladian admits a 1-dimensional decoherence free subspace our results suggest that finding a decoherence free subspaces is intractable for quantum computation. In addition, even the relaxed form of the problem, regarding finding slowly decaying pure states, where the decay rate is computed only up to first-order, is still intractable for quantum computation under the assumption BQP ̸ = QMA. There are a number of natural directions for future work. From a complexity-theoretic perspective, it would be interesting to determine whether the k-Local Lindbladian Pure Steady-State problem is QMA 1-Complete, which would require showing inclusion in QMA 1. Moreover, developing a perturbation gadget approach, similar to what was developed in [5], could prove useful in establishing further intractability results for Lindbladians which model more relevant physical systems. Another interesting open question is with regards to locality. Is the situation similar to the local Hamiltonian problem where the 2 -Local Hamiltonian problem is still QMA-Complete? Or is the situation something different, where the minimum locality to guarantee QMA-Hardness is greater than 2? From a more physical perspective, the intractability results established here motivate the need for finding more efficiently identifiable conditions that guarantee the presence or absence of a decoherence free subspace. Finally, as briefly mentioned in the introduction and expanded upon in [14], the decoherence free subspace is only one type of steady-state structure that time-independent Lindbladian dynamics admits. Given the QMA 1-Hardness of the decoherence free subspace problem, it could be interesting to explore the complexity theoretic consequences that the other types of Lindbladian steady-state structures imply.

Appendix A: Analysis of Propagation Dissipator 

We present an analysis of the propagation dissipator associated to a quantum circuit V as defined in Definition 6. More specifically, our goal is to provide an analysis of the dissipator’s kernel, its spectral gap as well as its leakage outside of the span of its own pure steady-states. Similar to what is done in the Feynman-Kitaev Hamiltonian construction, our first step is to “rotate away” the specifics of the quantum circuit our propagation dissipator is checking. To do so we define the circuit rotation for the quantum circuit V = UT −1UT −2 · · · U0 acting on register S

as the unitary 

U(V )(•) = U (V ) • U (V )† =

(1) After rotating away the specifics of the quantum circuit, we analyze the kernel and bound the gap of the resulting dissipator which call the rotated propagation dissipator. We can then rotate the kernel back and leverage the unitary invariance of the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product in order to answer the same questions about the original propagation dissipator. To analyze the leakage, we utilize rotated propagation dissipator in a similar fashion. In the remainder of this appendix we define our rotated propagation dissipator and connect it to a Laplacian of a graph. In the two sections following, we provide proofs of Propositions 4 and 5 giving us an analysis of the propagation dissipator’s kernel and its spectral gap. In the third and final section we prove Lemma 13 giving us a statement regarding the propagation dissipator’s leakage. 1. Characterization of Kernel 

Our strategy to characterize the kernel of the propagation dissipator involves characterizing the kernel of the rotated propagation disipator, then rotating the subspace back under the circuit rotation rotation unitary for the specific quantum circuit. To characterize the kernel of the rotated propagation dissipator our strategy is two fold. We first calculate the nullity of the rotated propagation dissipator. We subsequently construct a number of linearly independent steady-states equal to the nullity, therefore proving the kernel equal to the span of these steady-states. The graphs depicted in FIG 1 suggest that G has two connected components. It is known that the nullity for a graph’s Laplacian is equal to the number of connected components of its associated graph. Therefore FIG 1 suggest that dim ker ˜Dprop = 2 implying the nullity of the rotated propagation dissipator is also 2. To construct the linearly-independent steady-states of the rotated propagation dissipator, we take both the pure and mixed history states of the quantum circuit we want to embed and rotate them under the associated circuit rotation. We then take as an ansatz the portion of these operators supported on only the clock register. To proceed with our analysis we require the following statements regarding the graph associated to our rotated propagation dissipator. 

Lemma 19. Let G be the graph defined in Lemma 18 and Gm be the subgraph associated to vertices Vm = {(t, t )}T −1 

> t=0

,

Gm is a connected component of G.Proof. See Appendix B 

Lemma 20. Let G be the graph defined in Lemma 18 and Gp be the subgraph associated to vertices Vp = V \{ (t, t )}T −1 

> t=0

,

Gp is a connected component of G.33 

FIG. 1: Graphs associated with the rotated propagation dissipator for T = 3 and T = 4 

Proof. See Appendix B The above two Lemmas imply the following corollary regarding graph G.

Corollary 2. Let G be the graph defined in Lemma 18, then G has only two connected components. 

Using our connection between the rotated propagation dissipator ˜Dprop and the graph G associated to it, we can leverage the above statements regarding G in order to prove results about the rotated propagation dissipator. In the statement which follows we give a full characterization of the rotated propagation dissipator’s kernel and in the second we extend it to the propagation dissipator, proving Proposition 4.

3. Leakage 

With both the kernel and gap of our propagation dissipator analyzed we conclude this appendix by analyzing the leakage of our pure steady-state subspace out into the rest of the propagation dissipator’s kernel. More specifically, in the statement which follows, we upper-bound the overlap between the subspace associated to the pure steady-states of the propagation dissipator and the rest of its kernel. This allows a way to quantify how much the span of our pure steady-states leaks out into the rest of the propagation dissipator’s kernel.

Appendix B: Analysis of the Graph associated with the Rotated Propagation Dissipator 

Define the set of vertices V = {0, 1, · · · , T − 1}×2 and map P : V 7 → B (HC ) as P(tA,t B ) = |tA⟩ ⟨ tB |. As described in Lemma 18 of Appendix A, the rotated propagation dissipator expanded in the basis {Pv }v∈V is a Laplacian for a graph G over vertices V . Throughout Appendix A we leveraged various properties about this graph G in-order to prove statements about the rotated propagation dissipator. In this Appendix we provide an analysis of the graph G

defined in Lemma 18, providing proof of the various statements used throughout Appendix A. Before beginning our analysis it is useful to revisit some common definitions related to graph theory, for reference see [29]. A graph G can be defined as a tuple ( V, E ) where V contains the graph’s vertices and E ⊆  V

> 2

 its edges. 38 Set {v1, v 2} is in E if and only if there exists an edge connecting vertices v1 and v2. The degree of a vertex v ∈ V ,denoted deg( v) is equal to the number of edges connected to v.The Laplacian for a graph G is the matrix associated to the elements [LG]v1,v 2 =



deg( v1) if v1 = v2

−1 if v1̸ = v2 and {v1, v 2} ∈ E

0 otherwise (1) From the above definition of a Laplacian we can prove the following Lemma regarding when a matrix happens to be the Laplacian associated to a graph. 

Lemma 23. Let A be an n × n matrix If 1. A = A⊺

2. for all i̸ = j, matrix elements [A]ij ∈ {− 1, 0}

3. Pnj=1 [A]ij = 0 

then there exists a graph G over n vertices such that the Laplacian for graph G is matrix A.Proof. We first build the graph G associated to matrix A, then show the Laplacian for graph G is equal to A.Let V = [ n] be a set of vertices and define the set of edges E = {{ i, j } ∈  V

> 2

 | Aij = −1}. Consider the Laplacian associated to graph G = ( V, E ) denoted LG. Since |V | = n, LG is an n×n matrix. Let i, j ∈ V with i̸ = j. Since LG is a Laplacian, [ LG]ij ∈ {− 1, 0}. If [ LG]ij = −1 then {i, j } ∈ E implying Aij = −1 therefore [ LG]ij = Aij . In the other case [ LG]ij = 0 implying {i, j } /∈ E implying Aij ̸ = −1. Since i̸ = j, we must have Aij = 0 implying [ LG]ij = Aij as well. Thus for i̸ = j,[LG]ij = Aij . (2) Let i ∈ V . Since LG is a Laplacian Pnj=1 [LG]ij = 0 thus we have 

> n

> j=1

Aij . (5) which implies [ LG]ii = Aii . Thus for all i, j ∈ V

[LG]ij = Aij (6) implying the Laplacian for graph G is the n × n matrix A.Some other definitions that will be used include that of a subgraph, connectedness, and a component. Formally, let G be a graph with vertices V and edges E. If Vs ⊆ V , then the subgraph associated to Vs is the graph given by vertices Vs and edges  V

> 2

 ∩ E. A walk in G connecting vertices vs and vf is a finite sequence of edges {ei}Ni=1 ⊆ E

such that e1 contains vs, eN contains vf , and ei and ei+1 share a vertex. Similarly, a path is a walk that never revists an edge or vertex. Graph G is called connected if and only if for any pair of vertices vs, v f ∈ V there exists a walk in 

G connecting vs to vf . Conversely, let Gs be the subgraph associated to vertices Vs ⊆ V , Gs is called a component 

of G if and only if for all vertices vs ∈ Vs and vo ∈ V \ VS there does not exists a walk in G connecting vertices vs

and vo, ie Gs is disconnected from the rest of the graph. A subgraph is called a connected component if it is both a 39 component of G and a connected subgraph of G. In-addition, if G = ( V, E ) is a connected graph, the edge-connectivity 

of G is the size of the smallest set Ec ⊆ E such that the graph ( V, E \ Ec) is not connected. With these common definitions reviewed, we proceed to proving the following Lemmas characterizing the graph G

associated with the rotated propagation dissipator. Examples of G for various values of T can be found in FIG 1 of Appendix A. We begin by showing the “mixed component” of G depicted below, is indeed a connected component. We call this component “mixed” because the portion of the rotated propagation dissipator’s kernel generated by IC is associated to the kernel of this components Laplacian. Since IC generates the family of mixed history states when rotated back under our circuit rotation unitary discussed in Appendix A, this component of the G gives us the mixed history states associated to our propagation dissipator. 

FIG. 2: Mixed component of the graph associated to the rotated propagation dissipator for T = 4 

Lemma (Lemma 19 of Appendix A) . Let G be the graph defined in Lemma 18 and Gm be the subgraph associated to

Appendix C: Analysis of Input Dissipator 

The input dissipator Din defined in equation (55) of Definition 7 is used in our hardness construction in order to drive the system into a steady-state subspace with a properly initialized ancillary register. In this appendix we analyze 

Din , charcterizing its kernel inside the history state subspace, lower-bounding its spectral gap, as well as analyzing its leakage outside of it is pure steady-state subspace. These statements appear as Lemmas 11, 12, and 14 in the main text. In the first of the statements provided below, we characterizes the kernel of Din after it has been restricted to the subspace of pure history states.

Appendix D: Analysis of Unary Clock Dissipator 

The unary clock dissipator Dunary , defined in equation (106) of Definition 10 is used to drive our system into a steady-state subspace where the clock register exists in valid unary encoded clock states. This construction is useful when we extend our quasi-local hardness construction to become k-local in Section V. In this appendix we provide the proofs of Propositions 6, 7, and Lemma 16 which give us the statements characterizing the kernel of Dunary ,the spectral gap of Dunary , and the leakage of the pure steady-state subspace of Dunary out into the rest of its kernel respectively. The appendix is divided as follows. In the first section we prove Proposition 6 giving us a characterization of the unary clock dissipator’s kernel. In the second section we provide proof of Proposition 7 giving us a lower-bound on the gap of our unary clock dissipator. In the final section we prove Lemma 16 giving us an analysis of the leakage outside of our unary clock dissipators’ pure stead-state subspace. 47 

1. Characterization of Kernel 

Our general strategy to characterize the kernel of our unary clock dissipator follows an inductive argument over the number of qubits in our clock register C. We first prove the base case of our argument given in the following lemma.

2. Analysis of Spectral Gap 

Our argument for lower-bounding the spectral gap of our unary clock can be broken up into roughly two parts. The first part of our argument consists of block diagonalizing our dissipator across the partition B(HC ) = O+Dlegal +Dillegal

In-order to do so, we first argue that Dunary |Dillegal as matrix expanded in the computational basis can be interpreted as a transition-rate matrix for a continuous-time Markov chain. We then apply the standard canonical paths approach [] to argue for a lower-bound on the gap of this transition-rate matrix. A transition-rate matrix for a finite continuous-time Markov chain, sometimes called a Q-matrix , is defined as an 

|S| × | S| matrix Q, with matrix elements Qx,y satisfying 



0 ≤ − Qx,x < ∞

Qx,y ≥ 0 for x̸ = y

P 

> x∈S

Qy,x = 0 

. (137) A steady-state of the continuous-time chain is a probability distribution over S which satisfies Qπ = 0 For every transition-rate matrix, Q we can define a |S| × | S| stochastic matrix generating a discrete-time Markov chain over the state space S called a jump chain . The specific jump chain we will consider is generated by the stochastic matrix 

P = I + Q

∥Q∥ . (138) A steady-state of the discrete-time chain satisfies P π = π. Note, by construction our P defined above is positive semi-definite. A finite discrete-time Markov chain is called reversible if for a steady-state π we have Px,y πy = Py,x πx.In addition, it is sufficient to call the chain irreducible if it has a unique steady-state π that has full support over the state space S. For a good reference on both continuous-time and discrete-time Markov chains see [] In the statement to follow we prove that when Dunary is restricted to Dillegal then it becomes a transition-rate matrix for a continuous-time Markov chain over the state space of bit strings contained in S (T )illegal . This allows us to lower-bound the gap of Dunary restricted to D by lower-bounding the gap of the transition-rate matrix of the associated continuous-time Markov chain.
