(168) 66 To lower-bound the gap of the Q-matrix associated to Dunary |Dillegal we upper-bound the second largest eigenvalue of the stochastic matrix generating its jump chain defined in equation (138). To do so we use a canonical paths argument which requires analyzing the graph of allowable transitions our jump chain permits. We define the transition graph associated to a Markov chain generated by stochastic matrix P as a directed graph consisting of the state space as vertices and edges between vertices s1 and s2 only if Ps1,s 2 > 0. We define a set of 

canonical paths over a directed graph G as a set of paths Γ such that for all distinct vertices s1, s 2 in the graph G,there exists a unique path γs1,s 2 ∈ Γ connecting s1 to s2. Using a set of canonical paths over a transition graph, we can directly upper-bound the second largest eigenvalue associated to the stochastic matrix generating the Markov chain, to how much the set of canonical paths is congested. For the specific jump chain associated to the transition-rate matrix appearing in equation (139) of Lemma 35, we define our set of canonical paths over its transition graph as follows. Let x, y ∈ S(T )illegal and define indices 

s, f ∈ { 0, 1, · · · , T − 3} such that xs+2 xs+1 xs ∈ Sloc illegal and yf +2 yf +1 yf ∈ Sloc illegal where S loc illegal defined according to equation (103). If f − s ≥ 0 we define the path γx,y according to the sequence of vertices 



x → x>s +1 10 x<s → x>s 10 x<s −1 → · · · → x>110 Phase 1 

x>110 → x>210 y0 → x>310 y1y0 → · · · → 10 y<T −2 Phase 2 10 y<T −2 → yT −110 y<T −3 → y>T −310 y<T −4 → · · · → y>f +2 10 yf y<f → y Phase 3 

, (169) and if f − s < 0 we define γx,y according to the sequence of vertices 



x → x>s +2 10 x<s +1 → x>s +3 10 x<s +2 → · · · → 10 x<T −2 Phase 1 10 x<T −2 → yT −110 x<T −3 → yT −1yT −210 x<T −4 → · · · → y>110 Phase 2 

y>110 → y>210 y0 → y>310 y<2 · · · → y>f +2 yf +2 10 y<f → y Phase 3 

. (170) In both cases we assume cycles have been removed and edges only connect distinct vertices so that γx,y can form a valid path. In the statement which follows we prove γx,y is indeed a path in our transition graph. 

Lemma 37. Let G = (S (T )illegal , E ) be the transition graph associated to the jump chain of Q where Q is defined according to equation (139) of Lemma 35. Let x, y ∈ S(T )illegal and γx,y be the sequence of edges defined according to equations (169) and (170) , then γx,y is a path from x to y in G.Proof. Assume every e ∈ γx,y is contained in E. By construction γx,y is walk from vertex x to vertex y with all cycles removed, therefore it is a path. Thus it suffices to show that for all x, y ∈ S(T )illegal if e ∈ γx,y then e ∈ E. Since 

x, y ∈ S(T )illegal there exists at least one index s ∈ { 0, 1, · · · , T − 3} such that xs+2 xs+1 xs ∈ Sloc illegal , and similarly for y,there exists at least one index f ∈ { 0, 1, · · · , T − 3} such that yf +2 yf +1 yf ∈ Sloc illegal . Therefore, for γx,y there exists two cases, either f − s ≥ 0 or f − s < 0. Consider the case of f − s ≥ 0. γx,y is defined according to the sequence of vertices of equation (169). Let e ∈ γx,y ,we find three cases. Either e is defined from the Phase 1 portion of the sequence, the Phase 2 portion of the sequence, or the Phase 3 portion of the sequence. Consider the first of these three cases. Either e = ( x, x >s +1 10 x<s ) or there exists some t ∈ { 1, 2, · · · , s } such that e = ( x>t +1 10 x<t , x >t 10 x<t −1). If e = ( x, x >s +1 10 x<s ), since γx,y only contains edges between distinct vertices we have x̸ = x>s +1 10 x<s implying xs+2 xs+1 xs̸ = xs+2 10. Since xs+2 xs+1 xs ∈ Sloc illegal 

and xs+2 10 ∈ Sloc illegal we can apply Lemma 36 implying Qe ≥ 1/4. Thus we have Pe > 0 from equation (138) implying 

e ∈ E. Similarly, if e = ( x>t +1 10 x<t , x >t 10 x<t −1) for some t ∈ { 1, 2, · · · s} we can apply Lemma 36 as well, giving us 

Qe ≥ 1/4 and thus Pe > 0 implying e ∈ E. Consider the case of e ∈ γx,y being defined through the Phase 2 sequence of vertices. Thus there exists some index t ∈ { 0, 1, · · · T − 3} such that e = ( x>t +1 10 y<t , x >t +2 10 y<t +1 ). Similarly, we can apply Lemma 36 giving us Qe ≥ 1/4 implying Pe > 0 and thus e ∈ E. Finally, consider the case of e ∈ γx,y 

such that e is defined through the Phase 3 sequence of vertices given in equation (169). We have two cases, either there exists a t ∈ { f, f + 1 , · · · , T − 2} such that e = ( y>t +1 10 y<t , y >t 10 y<t −1) or e = ( y>f +1 10 y<f , y ). Since the edges only exist in γx,y if the vertices are distinct, and yf +2 yf +1 yf , y f +2 10 ∈ Sloc illegal we can apply Lemma 36 in both cases giving us Qe ≥ 1/4 and thus Pe > 0 implying e ∈ E. Therefore in the case of f − s ≥ 0, for all e ∈ γx,y , e ∈ E.Consider the case of f − s < 0. γx,y is defined according to the sequence of vertices of equation (170). Let e ∈ γx,y ,we find three cases again. Either e is defined from the Phase 1 portion of the sequence, the Phase 2 portion of the sequence, or the Phase 3 portion of the sequence. Consider the first of these three cases. Either e = ( x, x >s +2 10 x<s +1 )or there exists some t ∈ { s, s + 1 , · · · , T − 3} such that e = ( x>t +1 10 x<t , x >t +2 10 x<t +1 ). If e = ( x, x >s +2 10 x<s +1 ), since γx,y only contains edges between distinct vertices we have x̸ = x>s +2 10 x<s +1 implying xs+2 xs+1 xs̸ = 10 xs.Since xs+2 xs+1 xs ∈ Sloc illegal and 10 xs ∈ Sloc illegal we can apply Lemma 36 implying Qe ≥ 1/4. Thus we have Pe > 067 from equation (138) implying e ∈ E. Similarly, if e = ( x>t +1 10 x<t , x >t +2 10 x<t +1 ) for some t ∈ { s, s + 1 , · · · , T − 3}

we can apply Lemma 36 as well, giving us Qe ≥ 1/4 and thus Pe > 0 implying e ∈ E. Consider the case of e ∈ γx,y 

being defined through the Phase 2 sequence of vertices. Thus there exists some index t ∈ { 1, · · · T − 2} such that 

e = ( y>t +1 10 x<t , y >t 10 x<t −1). Similarly, we can apply Lemma 36 giving us Qe ≥ 1/4 implying Pe > 0 and thus 

e ∈ E. Finally, consider the case of e ∈ γx,y such that e is defined through the Phase 3 sequence of vertices given in equation (169). We have two cases, either there exists a t ∈ { 0, 1, · · · , f } such that e = ( y>t +1 10 y<t , y >t +2 10 y<t +1 )or e = ( y>f +2 10 y<f +1 , y ). Since the edges only exist in γx,y if the vertices are distinct, and yf +2 yf +1 yf , 10 yf ∈ Sloc illegal 

we can apply Lemma 36 in both cases giving us Qe ≥ 1/4 and thus Pe > 0 implying e ∈ E. Therefore in the case of 

f − s < 0, for all e ∈ γx,y , e ∈ E.In both cases we find for all e ∈ γx,y , we have e ∈ E, we conclude that γx,y is a path from x to y in G.In order to calculate the congestion associated to a set of canonical paths, we require both an upper-bound on the length of the longest path, as well as an upper-bound on the number of vertex pairs that use each edge contained in our set of paths. Intuitively, longer paths connecting vertices make it harder to move units of flow from one vertex to another using our paths. Similarly, the more a single edge is used by different vertex pairs, the more that edge can be overloaded by units of flow, thus congesting the network. In the first of the two statements provided below we upper-bound the path length while in the second we upper-bound the number of vertex pairs that use any single edge contained with in our paths. 

Lemma 38. Let G = (S (T )illegal , E ) be the transition graph associated to the jump chain of Q where Q is defined according to equation (139) of Lemma 35. Let x, y ∈ S(T )illegal and γx,y be the path defined according to equations (169) 

or (170) , then 

|γx,y | ≤ 3T. (171) 

Proof. Each γx,y is defined via the associated walk given by the sequence of vertices in either equation (169) or equation (170). Since removing cycles and self-edges only reduces the number of edges in the resulting sequence we find 

|γx,y | ≤ 3T. (172)

Appendix F: History State Decay under Quantum Circuit-SAT Dissipators 

A key feature in our proofs of the completeness and soundness of our reductions from Quantum Circuit-SAT to our various Local Lindbladian problems is our ability to relate the decay of a properly initialized history state, under the constructed Lindbladian, to the probability that the embedded quantum circuit accepts the input to the specific history state. Such a feature is captured in Lemmas 8 and 15 of Section V. In this appendix we provide the proofs of these two statements. The first being the statement of this key feature for the quasi-local Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator and the second for the local Quantum Circuit-SAT dissipator. 74 

Lemma (Lemma 8) . Let (V, b, a ) be an instance of Quantum Circuit-SAT and D(V,b,a ) defined according to equation (54) of Definition 7. Let |ηϕ⟩ be a pure history state associated to V with input state |ϕ⟩ = |ψ⟩M ⊗ | 0⟩A ∈HM ⊗ H A, then the decay of |ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ| under D(V,a,b ) is given by 

| ⟨| ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ| , Re D(V,a,b )(|ηϕ⟩ ⟨ ηϕ|)⟩ | =

Appendix G: Subspace Lemmas 

Throughout Section V we “project” our analysis through various different subspaces of the full pure state space associated to our Lindbladian dissipators which encode instances of Quantum Circuit-SAT . In this appendix we collect the various different Lemmas characterizing these pure state subspaces and provide their proofs.

Appendix H: The Symmetric* k-local Hamiltonian Problem 

In Section IV we introduce a new local Hamiltonian problem called the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamil-tonian problem which was used to provide a QMA verification protocol for our k-Local Lindbladian problem. In this appendix we give roof that this new local Hamiltonian problem is indeed in QMA by providing its QMA verification protocol. For reference we revisit the definition of the Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian 

problem given below. 

Definition (Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian Problem, Definition 4) . Let H be a 2k-local Hamil-tonian acting on 2n qubits partitioned into n qubit registers A and B with H given by {Hi}r=poly( n) 

> i=1

where I ⪰ Hi ⪰ 0

and if Hi acts non-trivially on k′ qubits in register A then it also acts non-trivially on k′ qubits in register B, decide 

• (accept-case) there exists a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ ∈ H A such that (⟨ψ|A ⊗ ⟨ ¯ψ|B )H(|ψ⟩A ⊗ | ¯ψ⟩B ) ≤ a,

• (reject-case) For all pure quantum states |ψ⟩ ∈ H A and |ϕ⟩ ∈ H B , (⟨ψ|A ⊗ ⟨ ϕ|B )H(|ψ⟩A ⊗ | ϕ⟩B ) ≥ b

promised one of these to be the case, where b − a ≥ 1/ poly( n).

The Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem is a variant of the Separable k-Local Hamil-tonian problem studied by Chailloux et al. in [18]. Thus we take inspiration from the QMA verification protocol provided for the Separable k-Local Hamiltonian problem in [18]. The QMA verification protocol queries the ver-ification protocol for the Consistency of k-Local Density Matrices problem for which we provide the definition of below. 

Definition 11 (Consistency Of k-Local Density Matrices [33]) . Let {ρi}m=poly( n) 

> i=1

be a set of k-local density matrices where each ρi acts on Ci ⊆ { 0, · · · , n − 1} qubits. Decide, 

• (accept-case) There exist an n-qubit state σ such that for all i ∈ [m], D(ρi, Tr {0,··· ,n −1}\ Ci [σ]) = 0 

• (reject-case) For all n-qubit states σ there exists an i ∈ [m] such that D(ρi, Tr {0,··· ,n −1}\ Ci [σ]) ≥ β,promised one of these to be the case, where β ≥ 1/ poly( n).79 

Algorithm 1 QMA Verification Protocol for Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian                                                      

> Input: An instance of Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian , ( F, a, b, A, B ), Merlin sends a classical description of the states {ρAi}mi=1 where ρAiacts on the kqubits in Athat Hi∈Facts on. Merlin also sends one quantum state |ψ⟩.
> 1: QA←Query Consistency of k-Local Density Matrices for {ρAi}mi=1 and β= ( b−a)/8m
> 2: if not QAthen
> 3: return: reject
> 4: E←Pmi=1 Tr( HiρAi⊗¯ρAi)
> 5: if E≤athen
> 6: return: accept
> 7: else
> 8: return: reject

An instance of Consistency of k-Local Density Matrices is given by a tuple ( S, β ) where β is defined as in Definition 11 and the set S consists of the k-local density matrices ρi described in Definition 11. It has been shown by Liu in [33] that Consistency of k-Local Density Matrices ∈ QMA and thus has a QMA verification protocol that can be queried non-adaptively in any other QMA verification protocol. Our verification protocol for 

Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian is provided below. Algorithm 1 is in essence the QMA verification protocol for Separable k-Local Hamiltonian found in [18], therefore our proof of the validity of the protocol follows similarly to that provided in [18]. Thus we show the following statement 

Proposition (Proposition 2) . For k ≤ log( n), Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian ∈ QMA .Proof. Consider the QMA verification protocal described in Algorithm 1. Each state ρi acts on 2 k qubits with 

k ≤ log( n), therefore each ρi is a poly( n) × poly( n) matrix implying line 4 can be computed in poly( n) time classical computation. Since Conistency of k-Local Density Matrice ∈ QMA line 1 compiles a poly( n) size quantum circuit. Since poly( n) time classical computation can be mapped to a poly( n) size quantum circuit, the whole protocol can be compiled into a poly( n) size quantum circuit and thus is efficient. 

Completeness: 

Let ( F, a, b, A, B ) be an (accept-case) instance of Symmetric* Separable k-Local Hamiltonian , thus there exists a pure quantum state |ψ⟩ ∈ H A such that (⟨ψ|A ⊗ ⟨ ¯ψ|B )H(|ψ⟩A ⊗ | ¯ψ⟩B ) ≤ a. (1) Merlin chooses ρAi = Tr A\Ai (|ψ⟩ ⟨ ψ|). Since ( {ρAi }mi=1 , β ) is an (accept-case) instance of Consistency of k-Local Density Matrices we have Pr( QA = accept) ≥ 2/3. (2) Thus the protocol does not reject on line 3 with a probability greater than 2 /3. From equation (1) we find
